### Page 11

Global Market Outlook 11

Our view

Foundation: Bond yields remain near multi-year highs, offering an attractive entry point. We view fixed income as a Core holding 
(ie, a Neutral allocation) in our multi-asset portfolios. Within the asset class, we maintain an Overweight stance on EM USD 
government bonds, given their attractive risk-reward. We hold an Underweight view on Developed Market (DM) government 
bonds due to the tail risks associated with term premia. 
The Fed’s decision to hold rates at 3.75-4.00% in October amid still-elevated energy prices suggests that every Fed meeting 
from here is ‘live’, with a rate change possible on incoming data. With US inflation still running hot and the labour market tight, 
we expect two more 25bps hikes (one by year-end and one in H1 2027), bringing the Fed’s target range for the federal funds 
rate to 4.25-4.5%. We continue to favour the 3-7-year part of the US government bond yield curve as the best hedge against 
any risk-off move driven by AI-related growth fears. As inflation pressures ease in H2 2027, we see room for the Fed to consider 
a rate cut, which should pull nominal yields lower. Solid corporate fundamentals and supportive technicals should keep spreads 
tight, but there is limited room for them to tighten further. We still favour EM USD over local-currency (LCY) government bonds. 
Opportunistic ideas: We are bullish on US TIPS, AAA-rated CLOs, broad US HYs and Australian dollar (AUD) corporate bonds.

DM rates – Underweight 
We remain Underweight DM government bonds. In the US, 
government bond yields have retraced meaningfully after a 
sharp bear steepening through September and early October. 
We expect real yields to stay elevated over the next six 
months as AI capex and productivity growth continue to drive 
above-trend economic growth. We expect two more 25bps 
rate hikes by June 2027.  
Over the next few months, we expect US government bond 
yields to remain rangebound. The near-term path hinges on 
five factors: 1) the 3 November US mid-term election, where 
a ‘Democratic sweep’ would likely be bond-supportive by 
constraining US President Trump’s fiscal spending agenda, 
while a ‘Republican sweep’ would likely weigh on bonds as 
markets price in further fiscal expansion and a higher term 
premium; 2) Fed communication at upcoming policy meetings; 
3) the US Treasury’s quarterly refunding announcement on 4 
November, where any signal of reduced long-end issuance 
would likely compress long-end yields; 4) softening auction 
demand and repatriation by foreign investors, which add 
uncertainty around demand for US government bonds; and 5) 
the fluid geopolitical situation in the Middle East, which is 
keeping oil prices elevated, with a risk of further upside. 
Going into H2 2027, we expect moderating oil prices and the 
fading inflationary impact of tariffs to give the Fed room to 
consider rate cuts. The US 30-year yield, however, is likely to 
stay elevated as fiscal deficit remains substantial next year.  
We expect a final ECB hike to 2.75% by December 2026, then 
a pause in H1 2027, followed by a 25bps cut in Q3 2027. In 
Japan, we expect quarterly pace of BoJ hikes, with one hike 
that takes the policy rate to 1.50% by December 2026 and two 
more in H1 2027, reaching 2.00% before pausing at mid-year.

7 Fixed Income – at a glance

Jonathan Liang, CFA 
CIO, Fixed Income & FX 
Cedric Lam 
Senior Investment Strategist 
Ray Heung 
Senior Investment Strategist 
Anthony Naab, CFA 
Investment Strategist

### Page 12

Global Market Outlook 12 
DM corporates – Core holding 
We maintain a Neutral allocation to both DM IG and DM HY 
corporate bonds, with a tactical bias to add exposure on 
episodic weakness, as carry continues to dominate total 
returns across DM credit. Credit spreads have widened from 
their lows but remain close to historical tights, while yields are 
still attractive, reinforcing a carry-led stance. This tight-spread 
regime is anchored by robust issuer fundamentals, strong 
profitability and resilient balance sheets, despite a visible 
moderation in rating upgrades over the past quarter.  
Technical supply dynamics are reshaping sector dispersion, 
especially in the IG space, where heavy AI-related issuance 
is weighing on the long end of the curve. AI-driven supply is 
surpassing USD 250bn in 2026 and making up a growing 
share of US IG gross issuance, but there are growing signs 
that hyperscalers are turning to private markets for funding, 
which should help ease public bond market supply pressures.  
We remain selective at the long end of the yield curve and 
favour a duration of 3-7 years, where risk-adjusted carry 
remains most durable. Issuance-driven concessions in 
sectors with strong capex cycles (notably technology, capital 
goods and basic industries) present opportunities for investors 
who can stomach near-term volatility. 
Fig. 14 DM IG spreads have widened from their lows, 
but remain near historically tight levels 
Bloomberg Global Aggregate Corp Index, option-adjusted 
spread (OAS)

Source: Bloomberg, Standard Chartered 
DM HY spreads have widened to roughly a six-month high. 
Persistent doubts about whether hyperscalers can generate 
adequate returns on massive AI capex investments triggered 
a broad risk asset sell-off. The CCC tier has become the focal 
point of genuine credit concern, with yields hovering near the 
high-15% range and spreads near a four-year high. Market 
focus has shifted to the funding pressures facing AI-related 
neocloud companies. We view this as idiosyncratic for now 
and see a limited impact on the broader HY universe. The 
underlying credit picture is mixed. Leverage is elevated and 
interest coverage is thin, but the broad market has not yet 
shown signs of systemic stress. The US HY default rate 
remains below trend in 2026. 
HY offers an attractive income proposition, with all-in yields of 
over 8%. Its shorter-duration profile, combined with its lower 
sensitivity to changes in the term premium and rate volatility, 
make it an attractive sleeve in the current phase of the cycle. 
We remain attentive to evolving credit rating migration trends 
and sector-specific supply pressures, particularly as upgrades 
slow and new issuance clusters around key themes. However, 
healthy issuer fundamentals and stable credit metrics 
continue to support it as a Core holding allocation. 
Fig. 15 DM HY spreads are off the tights 
Bloomberg Global HY Aggregate Corporate Index, OAS

Source: Bloomberg, Standard Chartered 
EM government bonds – Overweight 
USD/Core holding for LCY 
We are Overweight EM USD government bonds and retain 
EM LCY government bonds as a Core holding, reflecting a 
balanced perspective across both regional performance and 
currency dynamics. In EM USD sovereign debt, the recent 
spread widening offered an attractive entry point to gain 
exposure to areas benefiting from resilient global growth and 
stable external conditions. Technicals remain favourable, with 
supply being front-loaded and investor positioning remaining 
relatively light compared with historical levels. 
Many EM central banks having tightened earlier and more 
aggressively post-pandemic, preserving credibility and 
maintaining positive real rates. External balances have 
improved, reserve buffers have increased and greater 
reliance on domestic funding reduces external vulnerability. 
Lula’s surprise win in Brazil’s presidential election raises 
political risk. The country’s fiscal trajectory under the incoming 
administration is a key risk to monitor. Fiscal slippage could 
trigger a broader repricing of LATAM risk. In Asia, higher-rated 
issuers continue to offer a valuable buffer during bouts of 
global rate volatility, while select frontier exposures in EMEA 
remain more sensitive to liquidity shifts and changes in risk 
appetite. Index composition also shapes resilience: the EM 
USD benchmark is less exposed to oil price shocks, as 
countries that are not oil importers account for a larger share 
of the benchmark than in the LCY benchmark. We expect 
returns to be primarily driven by carry rather than further 
spread compression.

Global Market Outlook 13 
Fig. 16 EM USD government bonds offer a yield pick-up 
of nearly 3ppt over DM peers 
Bloomberg EM USD Government Bond Index, yield to worst

Source: Bloomberg, Standard Chartered 
EM LCY debt faces additional headwinds, especially as 
elevated US real yields and an appreciating USD dampen 
demand from foreign investors and reduce the comparative 
attractiveness of broad-based duration strategies. However, 
real yields remain comparatively supportive in several 
markets, and current valuations signal selective opportunities, 
especially given the restrictive monetary policy stances 
prevalent across many EM economies. Accordingly, EM LCY 
bonds retain their Core holding status, with portfolio emphasis 
placed on country selection and measured duration exposure, 
reflecting the differentiated currency dynamics at play. 
Key risks to both the USD and LCY segments include a 
potential escalation of the Middle East conflict, higher oil 
prices, renewed hawkishness from the Fed that could support 
further USD strength and weather-related disruptions, such as 
the effects of El Niño. Climate risks could prolong inflation 
pressures and hinder disinflation in vulnerable EM regions. 
Asia USD bonds – Core holding 
We retain a Neutral allocation to Asia USD bonds, an asset 
class that continues to reward investors with attractive 
nominal yields, favourable supply-demand technicals and 
robust credit fundamentals. Healthy corporate cash flows, low 
leverage and a high share of sovereign-linked issuers lend the 
segment a defensive quality relative to broader credit. 
The resilience has been evident year to date (YTD). Asia USD 
bonds have outperformed both DM government and DM IG 
corporate bonds on a total-return basis. We expect this 
relative strength to persist. The combination of carry and 
credit quality (over 60% of issues are sovereign or sovereign-
related) anchors our conviction in the asset class, even as we 
maintain a Neutral stance.

Bond opportunistic views 
Bullish US HY bonds 
We are opportunistically bullish on US HY bonds. We expect 
HY corporate earnings and cash flows to remain solid and 
leverage to remain stable. US HY bonds’ shorter-duration 
profile offers lower sensitivity to interest-rate volatility, which 
is an attractive characteristic in an environment of elevated 
term premia. 
Bullish US inflation-protected bonds (TIPS) 
Real yields remain elevated versus historical standards, 
driven by rate hike expectations. Inflation concerns persist as 
energy prices remain subject to upside risks. We believe TIPS 
offer protection against near-term inflation uncertainty.   
Bullish AUD corporate bonds 
The threshold for further RBA hikes has risen, as higher 
energy prices weigh on the growth outlook and complicate the 
policy trade-off. The transmission channel matters too, where 
higher long-end yields could feed through to the property 
market, tightening financial conditions without the RBA having 
to act, casting further doubt on the case for additional hikes. 
Technicals have improved slightly as higher yields slow new 
supply. Supply remains a tail risk, though. The AUD bond

market is much smaller than the USD market, especially at the 
long end of the curve, where demand for duration is thinner 
and the term premium is most sensitive to supply. 
Bullish AAA-rated CLOs 
Concerns about spillovers from private credit are mounting 
amid deteriorating covenant protections in parts of the 
leveraged credit market. However, we believe AAA-rated 
CLOs backed by solid asset portfolios and substantial credit 
enhancements underneath senior tranches should help 
dampen credit risk and volatility. We see a compelling case 
for floating-rate and shorter-duration alternatives as portfolio 
diversifiers amid elevated term premia.  
Close utilities corporate hybrids 
AI capex is set to keep rising, but funding it has become 
harder. Tighter financial conditions now coincide with growing 
competition for capital. Utilities' credit fundamentals remain 
strong. Even so, we close our utilities corporate hybrids idea 
to lock in a gain of 0.5% as a risk-management step.

### Page 14

Global Market Outlook 14

Our view

We downgrade global equities from Overweight to a Core allocation as we expect higher volatility in the near term. We 
expect this volatility to arise as equity market leadership broadens during the transition to a mid-cycle bull market. Equity returns 
will likely moderate from the 15-20% annualised range of the last few years but remain positive. We remain Overweight US and 
AxJ equities, which are expected to deliver the highest earnings growth in 2027 among the major regions. 
Within AxJ, we remain Overweight Taiwan, given structural AI-driven growth, and upgrade India to Overweight, for diversified 
non-AI growth exposure. South Korea is a Core holding, with attractive valuations but extreme volatility. China is also a Core 
holding, with attractive valuations but lacking a positive catalyst. We are Underweight ASEAN, which has a weaker growth profile. 
We maintain Japan as a Core holding as nominal growth supports broad-based earnings revisions. We also maintain a Core 
allocation to Europe ex-UK, which is enjoying an earnings recovery. We remain Underweight UK equities, given the market’s 
limited exposure to growth sectors.

8 Equity – at a glance

Sundeep Gantori, CFA, CAIA 
Chief Investment Officer, Equities 
Fook Hien Yap 
Senior Investment Strategist 
Cindy Lam, CFA 
Senior Investment Strategist

### Page 15

Global Market Outlook 15

Shifting to a mid-cycle bull market  
The last few years have seen strong annual returns of 15-20% 
for global equities, consistent with the early cycle of a bull 
market, driven in large part by a rapid AI buildout. As the AI 
infrastructure buildout is increasingly debt funded – we 
estimate 40% of AI capex in 2027 would be debt-financed – 
the market is increasingly concerned about the quality of this 
growth and execution risks. We remain positive on the 
structural AI theme, but we expect investor focus to shift from 
rapid buildout to monetisation. During this transition, we 
expect market leadership to broaden beyond technology. 
We believe this shift will also be accompanied by higher near-
term volatility and a moderation in global equity returns, 
consistent with a transition to a mid-cycle bull market.  
Fig. 18 Projected 16% earnings growth for global 
equities in 2027 provides a strong tailwind for positive 
equity returns  
2026 and 2027 consensus earnings growth estimates for 
MSCI AC World sectors

Source: FactSet, Bloomberg, Standard Chartered  
US equities – Overweight  
US equity valuations have declined this year, with the 12-
month forward P/E falling to 19x from 22x at the start of the 
year. This reflects higher rates, a valuation headwind, and 
worries about the quality of growth, which is increasingly debt-
funded. As market leadership broadens and the US mid-term 
election nears, we expect higher near-term volatility in the US 
market. Nonetheless, US equities have broad-based strength, 
with the ongoing Q3 earnings season pointing to a robust 
economy. In a world of rising geopolitical risks, the US is also 
relatively insulated by its domestically driven growth. 
AxJ equities – Overweight 
AxJ has the strongest earnings growth among major 
markets, as its technology sector remains a key beneficiary of 
the AI capex cycle. AxJ’s P/E valuations have also dropped to 
attractive trough levels, the lowest among major regions. 
Within AxJ, we are Overweight Taiwan, which benefits from 
clear visibility on AI-driven growth and dominance in 
manufacturing advanced AI chips. We also upgrade India to  
Overweight due to its resilient domestically driven earnings, 
providing diversification beyond AI-related growth. India offers 
the highest exposure to defensive sectors (healthcare, 
consumer staples and utilities) within AxJ. Both Taiwan and 
India offer attractive returns on equity.  
South Korea equities remain a Core holding. We are positive 
on long-term agreements that improve earnings visibility for 
memory chipmakers. Valuations are attractive (the lowest in 
AxJ), with supportive corporate governance reforms. Still, 
extreme volatility remains the key overhang. China remains 
a Core holding, given inexpensive valuations, but re-rating 
potential appears delayed amid AI monetisation struggles, 
lingering regulatory risks and weak domestic demand. We 
remain Underweight ASEAN, as Indonesia’s downgrade 
warning, oil shocks and a well-supported USD could weigh on 
the region, although Singapore offers defensive growth. 
Fig. 19 India offers highest AxJ exposure to defensive 
sectors (healthcare, consumer staples and utilities) 
Sector weights for MSCI equity indices

Source: FactSet, Bloomberg, Standard Chartered 
Japan equities – Core allocation 
Rising nominal growth is supporting solid broad-based 
earnings revisions, with rising rates supportive of bank 
earnings. Corporate governance reforms also continue to 
boost shareholder value. The fiscal support for strategic 
industries is pro-growth, offsetting energy shocks.  
Europe ex-UK equities – Core allocation  
Europe ex-UK is seeing an earnings recovery after three 
stagnant years, and we expect to see a continuation of this 
narrative with Q3 results. Rising rates are supportive of banks’ 
earnings and fiscal expansion is benefiting domestic demand. 
The fiscal situation in France has no easy solution, but we do 
not expect a contagion widening in government bond spreads, 
as the ECB has tools in place to limit contagion.  
UK equities – Underweight  
The UK lacks exposure to growth sectors, with relatively 
muted earnings growth. We expect this to weigh on the UK, 
making it lag the performance of other markets over the next 
12 months, although UK equities tend to hold up better during 
bouts of volatility.

Global Market Outlook 16

Add MSCI Switzerland and US Healthcare, 
remove Global Power & Electrification, MSCI 
Taiwan, Global Semis and US Comm Services 
We initiate an opportunistic Idea on MSCI Switzerland on 29 
October 2026, having initiated one on US Healthcare on 12 
October 2026. Healthcare and Consumer Staples account for 
over 50% of the MSCI Switzerland Index’s weight, providing 
exposure to more resilient demand. It also has a sizeable 
financial sector supported by steady wealth management 
growth and well-capitalised insurers, which benefit from 
disciplined underwriting and resilient premium income. 
We closed our opportunistic ideas in Global 
Semiconductors (9 September 2026 to 2 October 2026: 
+8.1%), MSCI Taiwan (9 July 2026 to 2 October 2026: 
+10.0%), US Communication Services (18 June 2026 to 12 
October 2026: +1.9%), and Global Power & Electrification 
(30 July 2026 to 29 October 2026: -6.5%). This reflects a 
tactical reduction in exposure to tech-related sectors amid 
higher expected near-term volatility, rather than a 
deterioration in the fundamental outlook. Over the longer term, 
we remain constructive on these structural themes and may 
revisit these ideas at more attractive entry points.

Ongoing Opportunistic Ideas 
US Healthcare: We expect US Healthcare to offer resilience 
amid shifting market leadership, with the sector projected to 
grow by 23% in 2027, the second-fastest growth rate among 
MSCI US sectors, underpinned by healthcare innovation. 
Euro area banks: A ‘higher-for-longer’ rate backdrop should 
extend the earnings upcycle, with further ECB tightening 
expected. Corporate loan demand is showing recovery, while 
non-interest income is growing. Strong capital generation 
supports higher dividends and continued share buybacks. 
Japan Banks: BoJ policy normalisation drives net interest 
margin upside, while Japan’s reflation cycle supports nominal 
loan demand, corporate capex and household wealth activity. 
Governance reform is a re-rating driver independent of the 
rate cycle. This includes recycling legacy holdings towards 
growth, buybacks and dividends.  
Global High Dividend: Provides a higher-income equity 
allocation with historically lower volatility than the MSCI ACWI 
and modest downside cushioning on balance.  
Sector views: Continue to broaden & diversify 
We made two changes on 12 October 2026: we downgraded 
US Communication Services to Neutral to reduce 
concentration in tech-related sectors and upgraded US 
Healthcare to Overweight, where its defensive exposure 
stands to benefit as investors diversify beyond concentrated 
AI exposure. This month, we downgrade Europe ex-UK 
Industrials to Neutral, as energy-cost shocks and supply-
chain pressures outweigh fiscal support. Elsewhere, US 
Information Technology (IT) remains Overweight. Rising 
funding and execution risks warrant near-term caution, but 
structural AI tailwinds should continue to provide long-term 
growth opportunities. US Materials also stays Overweight 
on sustained demand for industrial metals and construction 
materials. We remain bullish on gold. The Financial sector 
remains Overweight across the US, Europe ex-UK and 
Japan, supported by stable earnings, elevated rates boosting 
net interest income and continued capital market recovery. 
In China, we maintain a broad Overweight stance to preserve 
sector diversification. IT and Communication Services 
remain Overweight, with rapid innovation and investment in 
AI localisation providing tailwinds. Beyond these, we are also 
Overweight Materials on structurally elevated commodity 
prices, Healthcare on biotech innovation and a robust drug 
pipeline and the Financial sector on easing margin pressure, 
recovering fee income and capital market reform.

Global Market Outlook 17

Our view

• We remain Overweight gold, but trim our three- and 12-month price targets to USD 4,350/oz and 4,700/oz, respectively. 
• We retain our three- and 12-month West Texas Intermediate (WTI) oil forecast at USD 100/bbl and USD 80/bbl, respectively.

Gold outlook: We remain constructive over 6-12 months, 
viewing the latest pullback as liquidity-driven rather than a 
deterioration in fundamentals. USD strength is a headwind, 
but gold’s sensitivity to real yields has eased amid continued 
central bank buying, led by China, and resilient investor 
demand. Speculative positioning near May lows raises scope 
for renewed inflows. High oil prices sustain near-term volatility 
but may eventually lift gold if they constrain global growth.

Oil outlook: Crude prices remain elevated as shipping 
disruptions drive freight costs to multi-year highs, sustaining a 
geopolitical risk premium. While Middle East exports have 
proven resilient, threats to production infrastructure are 
mounting. Refined products, especially diesel, face the 
greatest supply pressure amid depleting inventories and 
constrained refining capacity. With limited emergency supply 
buffers, oil markets remain vulnerable to prolonged disruption.

15 Gold, crude oil – at a glance

Anthony Naab, CFA 
Investment Strategist

### Page 18

Global Market Outlook 18

USD view

USD consolidation with limited upside: Our three-month US Dollar Index (DXY) forecast stands at 101, revised upwards from 
100.20 last month, reflecting a higher expected Fed rate path. With the October Fed rate pause and a potential December rate 
hike largely priced in, further tightening would offer limited additional USD support. Persistent inflation, elevated US government 
bond yields and resilient business activity continue to underpin the USD's interest-rate advantage. However, in September, 
payroll growth slowed sharply to 29,000 (from 133,000 in August), the unemployment rate rose to 4.2% from 4.1%, annual wage 
growth eased to 3.0% from 3.1% and housing starts fell 5.2% m/m. These signs of cooling demand temper expectations of more 
aggressive Fed tightening. We expect DXY to remain rangebound between 101 and 103, with our three-month forecast at 101.00. 
Peaking Fed rates and narrowing rate differentials favour gradual USD weakness: We maintain our 12-month DXY forecast 
at 98.50. We expect a Fed rate hike in December 2026 and another in H1 2027, taking the federal funds target range to a peak 
of 4.25-4.50%, before easing in H2 2027. Further BoJ normalisation and the ECB's relatively restrictive policy stance should 
progressively narrow the USD's rate advantage. Persistent fiscal and current account deficits increase reliance on foreign capital 
inflows, adding to medium-term pressure on the USD. Resilient US growth, AI-related capital inflows and elevated US 
government bond yields remain important offsets, supporting DXY’s gradual decline towards 98.50.

EUR/USD 1.1202 1.14 1.17 
Constructive: French fiscal risks and high US bond yields weigh in the near term. 
Germany's multi-year investment programme, resilient exports and a narrowing US 
rate advantage support medium-term EUR recovery.

GBP/USD 1.3374 1.33 1.35 
Rangebound: Higher UK inflation and stronger October PMI support BoE tightening 
expectations. While Healey's plan deferred the biggest fiscal decisions rather than 
resolving them, any future fiscal slippage or gilt market stress would limit GBP gains.

USD/JPY 156.87 157 153 
Rangebound with bearish bias: Further BoJ tightening, lower US rates and 
moderating oil prices support the JPY. However, negative Japanese real rates and 
overseas investment flows could limit the pace of JPY appreciation.

AUD/USD 0.7036 0.72 0.74 
Constructive: Weak employment and a contracting October PMI temper near term 
AUD gains. However, the RBA’s restrictive policy, resilient commodity demand and 
a narrowing US rate advantage support gradual AUD appreciation.

NZD/USD 0.5699 0.56 0.57 
Rangebound: Q3 inflation eased to 3.7% y/y from 4.1%, reducing pressure for 
additional RBNZ tightening. Subdued domestic growth and elevated US yields 
favour NZD consolidation towards 0.56 in the near term, before a softer medium-
term USD supports a recovery towards 0.57.

USD/CAD 1.4003 1.40 1.37 
Rangebound with bearish bias: US-Canada trade uncertainty and softer 
Canadian employment limit near-term CAD gains. We expect the Fed to ease in H2 
2027, narrowing the US interest rate advantage and supporting gradual CAD 
appreciation.

USD/CHF 0.8250 0.82 0.80 
Modestly bearish: French fiscal uncertainty supports CHF safe-haven inflows, 
outweighing the wide US-Swiss rate differential, favouring appreciation towards 0.82 
near-term. Fading US rate support from H2 2027 favours further appreciation; zero 
SNB rates and CHF’s funding role are likely to keep CHF appreciation pace gradual.

USD/CNH 6.6690 6.62 6.52 
Modestly bearish: September exports grew 36.3% y/y and the trade surplus 
widened to USD 133.5bn, strengthening exporter USD conversion support. 
Industrial production growth accelerated to 5.5%, while retail sales growth remained 
subdued at 1.1%. PBoC fixing guidance and strong external flows support controlled 
CNH appreciation towards 6.52.

USD/SGD 1.2781 1.28 1.26 
Near-term rangebound; medium-term modestly bearish: The Monetary Authority 
of Singapore maintained its S$NEER appreciation policy unchanged in October, 
following a “very slight” increase in July. Q3 GDP expanded 5.4% y/y, supported by 
technology-related activity. The unchanged appreciating policy band and narrowing 
medium-term US rate advantage favour gradual SGD appreciation towards 1.26.

USD/INR 94.94 – 96 
Modestly constructive: October composite PMI strengthened to 56.7 from 55.9, 
while the RBI raised its repo rate by 25bps to 5.50%, supporting the INR through 
tighter monetary policy. However, September's USD 30.2bn trade deficit, elevated 
oil import costs and external funding needs sustain USD demand, favouring gradual 
INR depreciation towards 96.

USD/MYR 4.0857 – 4.00 
Modestly bearish: September exports grew 54.5% y/y and the trade surplus 
widened to MYR 33.7bn, strengthening export-related FX conversion. Resilient GDP 
growth of 5.3% y/y and a softer medium-term USD support gradual MYR 
appreciation towards 4.00.

USD/KRW 1,360 – 1,450 
Bullish: Strong semiconductor exports support Korea's external surplus, but 
concerns over AI investment returns and concentrated technology equity exposure 
have increased foreign portfolio selling. Overseas investment and the US-South 
Korea yield advantage sustain USD demand, supporting our forecast for USD/KRW 
to rise towards 1,450.

### Page 20

Global Market Outlook 20

Summary

Our stock-bond model (3-6 months) is at -2 (minimum: -5) in October, signalling a modest Underweight (UW) in global 
equities. Historically, a decline in the model score to -2 has been associated with flat to slightly negative returns. The shift was 
driven by stretched valuations in both global and Asian equities, weaker fundamentals and deteriorating market technicals. On 
fundamentals, market risk aversion has risen sharply, while PMI new orders have declined over the past three months, pointing 
to a slowdown in the pace of industrial expansion. On technicals, the share of equity markets trading above their 200-day moving 
averages, a key gauge of long-term support, has fallen below 60% for the first time since January 2025. The model rebalances 
in the middle of each month and has generated 11bps of alpha from the equity Underweight initiated on 15 October 2026, lifting 
YTD alpha vs. the 60/40 equity-bond benchmark to 4.0%.  
Our 1-3-month equity market regime model indicates contained bear market risks across US and global equities. 
Estimated bear market probabilities are 4.2% for the S&P500 and 7.7% for the MSCI AC World, compared with 0.9% and 6.4% 
respectively in the September outlook. Across both models, volatility measures are the main factors keeping probabilities low, 
supported by momentum signals. Together, these outweigh modest upward contributions from macro and cross-market factors, 
leaving little evidence of a bear market regime over the next 1-3 months.  
Our market diversity indicator flags reversal risk in MSCI Europe Real Estate and MSCI China Industrials, which are down 
over 12% and 19% respectively from their August peaks. US equity and USD positioning remain broadly neutral.

15 Quant perspective: Modestly UW

Francis Lim Senior Quantitative Strategist Maggie, Au Yeung Quantitative Analyst

### Page 21

Global Market Outlook 21 
Fig. 33 Long- and short-term quantitative models remain bullish on risk assets 
Long-term models below have a typical time horizon of 3-6 months, while short-term models have a 1-3-month horizon

Long-term Stock or bond US recession model 
Current view Modest equity underweight 25% probability within the next 6 months 
What factors 
is this view 
based on? 
• Fundamental: +1. Fundamentals weakened, with 
macro risk indicator rising above 0.5 threshold and 
the negative three-month change in PMI new orders 
pointing to a slowdown in industrial activity 
expansion 
• Valuation: -2. Both DM and Asian equities are 
expensive as their valuation scores exceeded the 
model’s 1 z-score threshold 
• Technical: -1. The percentage of equity markets 
above their 200-day moving averages has fallen 
steeply from above 80% since in August to current 
47% 
Estimated probability rose slightly from 21%  
• Conference board consumer confidence 
worsened to -14.3% y/y 
• PMI new orders to inventory ratio is at 1.14x, 
below its historical average of 1.2x 
• Conference board leading indicators improved 
but remained negative at -0.7% y/y 
• Fed funds rate to natural rate ratio is still high 
at 1.7x, reflecting a restrictive monetary policy 
compared to historical average of 1.3x 
Key model 
factors 
• Economic activity, macro risk and surprise indices, 
corporate earnings, forward P/E ratio and technical 
factors 
• Conference Board leading indicator, Consumer 
Confidence Index, ISM New Orders and Fed 
funds rate 
How does it 
work? 
• A monthly scorecard of -5 to 5 based on 
fundamentals, valuations and market breadth 
factors to indicate the relative preference for bonds 
and equities. A positive score favours equities and 
vice versa 
• A probability model based on regression. Long-
term statistical tests are conducted to identify 
the four key economic factors above

Short-term Technical analysis Investor positioning 
Current views Bullish S&P 500 & MSCI AC World MSCI Europe Real Estate and China Industrials 
What factors 
is this view 
based on? 
• Bullish S&P500 and MSCI AC World. Bear market 
probabilities remain low at 4.2% and 7.7% 
respectively. Volatility measures are the main factors 
keeping these estimates low, supported by 
momentum signals and outweighing modest 
contributions from macro and cross-market factors 
• Market diversity indicator flags reversal 
risk in Europe Real Estate and China 
Industrials. The indices have declined by 
more than 12% and 19%, respectively, from 
their August peaks 
• Investor positioning remains broadly neutral 
across both US equities and the US dollar, 
indicating that investor exposure is not 
especially stretched in either direction 
Key model 
factors 
• Market factors: Momentum, volatility, interest rate 
differentials, relative returns, inflation swap rates, 
economic surprises, etc 
• Price action: Overbought conditions occur 
when prices rise sharply; oversold conditions 
happen when prices fall rapidly in a short time 
How does it 
work? 
• Scanning through 7,000+ factors, the framework 
uses ML to forecast market regimes or future trends 
based on identified market drivers 
• A market indicator based on fractal analysis 
that provides a timely indication of investor 
positioning based on price actions

Time is your most precious commodity – be sure to spend it wisely 
Time is undoubtedly valuable. The days may seem long, but the years are short. So, make the choice 
to spend your time wisely. Whether you’re setting out on your investment journey, navigating the 
intricacies of mid-life wealth planning or fortifying assets for your golden years, invest time today to 
ensure your wealth strategy is aligned to what’s right for you – Today, Tomorrow and Forever. 
Setting aside the time now to review your plan will pay dividends in the future. Markets have moved. 
Your portfolio’s current asset allocation may no longer be optimally positioned to maximise the 
opportunities ahead. Ask yourself the following: Am I holding too much cash? Am I sufficiently allocating 
to growth assets for the long term? Is my portfolio diversified? Am I capturing the best opportunities? 
And most importantly, is my wealth working hard for me so that I don’t have to? 
Use our SC Wealth Select framework and advisory specialists to help guide you through this process.

Principles 
that stand the 
test of time 
Adhering to time-tested principles, to ensure your 
investment decisions remain robust and 
consistently applied, is paramount to your 
success Today, Tomorrow and Forever. We use 
five Wealth Principles to guide and guardrail your 
wealth decisions.

Discipline – ensure consistency and 
prudence over your emotions 
• Reacting to emotions, such as optimism and 
fear, can lead to poor investment decisions at 
the worst times 
• Have a plan and stick to it – this helps you to 
stay focused on the bigger picture

Diversification – simply put, don’t put 
all your eggs in one basket 
• Reduce risk by holding a variety of financial 
assets. Multi-asset diversification in your 
Foundation portfolio is important 
• As a guide, make sure your portfolio contains 
a variety of asset classes and investments 
that have low correlation with one another

Time in the Market – a more robust 
strategy than timing the market

• Predicting market selloffs is challenging, and 
timing your exit and re-entry is difficult 
• Missing out on the best performing days of a 
market can have a significantly detrimental 
impact on your portfolio 
• ‘Time in the market’ and buying the market 
with a longer-term view provide more 
consistent returns that can ride out bumps 
along the way

Risk and Return – make sure the risk 
is worth the return 
• To achieve higher investment returns, you will 
likely have to accept a greater level of risk in 
your portfolio 
• Therefore, it’s important to understand the 
risks and manage these on an ongoing basis

Protection – don’t let the unexpected 
catch you unprepared 
• Even though you may feel healthy, or 
financially stable now, protection offers the 
ability to overcome times of financial 
uncertainty and mitigate the long-term impact 
of unforeseen events on your wealth 
• A good protection plan not only safeguards 
your wealth today, but also considers the 
value of your future earnings over your 
lifetime, in today’s terms

## Document 455: Why hasn’t there been a major financial crisis in recent years_ (2026-11-03)

Source PDF: all report pdf/Why hasn’t there been a major financial crisis in recent years_ (2026-11-03).pdf
Report source: DB Research
Date: 2026-11-03
Pages: 19
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

Deutsche Bank 
Research Institute

Why hasn’t there been a major 
financial crisis in recent years?

1 
Authors 
Henry Allen 
Macro Strategist

Peter Sidorov, CFA 
Senior Economist

Camilla Siazon 
Research Analyst

November 3, 2026 
— The economist Hyman Minsky warned, in essence, that “stability is destabilising”. He 
argued that a lengthy period of financial stability could be problematic, as it induced 
risk-taking and complacency amongst investors, laying the foundation for the next 
crisis. For example, lenders relax their terms and borrowers are more willing to extend 
themselves, as nobody wants to miss out. This dynamic makes economies inherently 
prone to financial panics and boom-bust cycles. 
— But since the 2008 crisis, and the subsequent sovereign crisis in the Eurozone, we 
have yet to see another financial crisis happen on a scale that had a lasting market 
and macroeconomic impact. There have been clear moments of concern, as in early 
2023 with the regional bank turmoil, but nothing that has caused a lasting impact. So 
this led us to ask why. 
— In this paper, we examine why the post-GFC era has seen fewer financial crises. 
Explanations include the reforms made after 2008 to avoid a repeat, the lower 
proportion of financial assets held by the banking system, as well as the speedier 
reaction of policymakers when wobbles have occurred. We then consider a few 
candidates that could cause the next crisis, including AI vulnerabilities, private credit, 
and stretched fiscal positions. 
— Ultimately, financial crises haven’t been abolished, but the reforms of recent years 
have made it harder for shocks to propagate through the traditional banking system. 
Moreover, policymakers have become far more inclined to intervene when initial signs 
of stress have emerged.

### Page 2

3 November 2026
Thematic Research
Page 2 Deutsche Bank AG
Section 1: What is a financial crisis, and what causes them?
There isn’t a single technical definition of a “financial crisis”, and they come in many
different types. Commonly, we think of banking crises like 2008, but they can also
encompass currency/balance of payments crises, debt crises and other forms too.
Given currency and sovereign crises have been more common in emerging markets
(the UK's ERM crisis in 1992 and the euro crisis being the two most notable DM

occurrences), historical analysis in developed economies has tended to focus
heavily on banking crises. Non-bank funding or market-liquidity crises are often not
categorised separately in the literature, but they have become more common in
recent years. This includes the "dash-for-cash" stress at the onset of the Covid
pandemic in March 2020 and the 2022 LDI crisis in the UK.
But for all the different types of financial crisis, there are common themes between

them. Often, they are accompanied by asset bubbles that burst in a non-linear
fashion. We generally see a severe tightening in credit conditions, which in turn
flows through into a broader economic contraction. Frequently, there are
contagion-like dynamics that can be difficult to stop. And in the modern world, they
usually require policymakers to step in, stabilising the situation with interventions.
At risk of oversimplification, we note four factors that determine the emergence and

the severity of a financial crisis. The first three are well-established in the financial
literature, sometimes pooled together into a build-up and a crisis phase
(Brunnermeier & Oehmke 2013), while the fourth has become increasingly
important over the past 15-20 years:
1.  Underlying imbalances. Bubbles and financial imbalances build during
the run-up phase, leaving accumulated risks that materialise when the
crisis erupts. This is the build-up typically described by the early phases of

Kindleberger's model of financial crises.
2.  Trigger. This is the shock that turns an existing vulnerability into clear
financial stress. These are often very difficult to predict and we mostly leave
these outside the focus of this report.
3.  Amplification and financial system fragility. Various factors determine
whether a shock propagates through the financial system, and the
intensity of that shock. These include leverage, liquidity and maturity

mismatches, interconnectedness, and the concentration of exposures.
Other channels in the financial system can then amplify the shock,
including bank runs, fire sales and margin calls, and negative net worth
effects, sovereign–bank loops etc.
4.  Intervention/policy response. This has been increasingly important in
recent decades as the size and scope of policy response to crises has
increased. The outright size can vary, and intervention can occur across

several dimensions, ranging from direct intervention in financial markets,
to counter-cyclical macroeconomic policy that mitigates the impact on the
real economy.
Clearly, these factors are not independent and the interaction between them is what
determines the severity of a financial crisis.

### Page 3

3 November 2026
Thematic Research
Deutsche Bank AG Page 3
One important aspect is the joint role that underlying imbalances and amplification
play. While theoretical models such as Diamond & Dybvig highlight the potential for
a bank run that triggers a broad crisis, empirical research shows that in practice, it
is exceedingly rare for a bank run to lead to bank failure in the absence of an
underlying solvency issue (Correia, Luck and Verner 2026). In other words, the

presence of amplification channels alone does not lead to a crisis.
Conversely, in the absence of amplification, it is possible to get significant
turbulence in asset prices, but without a financial crisis. For instance, a bear market,
wider credit spreads, or even a recession do not in and of themselves imply there
is a financial crisis. This was seen clearly with the bursting of the dot com bubble,
a severe equity decline but not a financial crisis, though it did cause a recession. At

the same time, in the chart below, we can see several US equity bear markets that
took place outside of recessions. On other occasions, however, a “Minsky
Moment” can then happen, where even a seemingly minor event can trigger a high-
profile incident that cascades across the financial system.
Figure 1: S&P 500 drawdowns over time, with shaded recessions showing select episodes when the index fell over 20%
without coinciding with a recession
Source :Bloomberg Finance LP, NBER, Deutsche Bank Research

Looking back through history, we can see how variation in these factors has led to
different regimes over time in terms of frequency/severity of financial factors.

### Page 4

3 November 2026
Thematic Research
Page 4 Deutsche Bank AG
Section 2. Why have financial crises been more or less likely
in certain periods of history?
1. Pre-WWII: More frequent financial instability, albeit under a very
different system.
Financial instability was a recurrent feature of the pre-WWII era. During the 19th
and early 20th centuries, there were frequent panics that often led to major slumps.
For instance, the Panic of 1873 sparked a period often known as the “Long

Depression”, which extended into the 1890s for many countries.
In part, financial panics occurred more frequently because policy operated under
very different settings. For instance, the Gold Standard as the monetary system
constrained a countercyclical policy response and transmitted deflation
internationally, while falling prices increased real debt burdens. Moreover, the
Federal Reserve wasn’t established until 1913, and the lack of an effective lender of

last resort meant there was a private intervention during the US Panic of 1907. The
first wave of globalisation also brought new vulnerabilities. Between 1870 and
1913, many emerging economies accumulated debts denominated in gold or
foreign currency, leaving them exposed when capital inflows reversed or exchange
rates weakened.
The biggest and most famous crisis occurred in 1929, when the Wall Street Crash
of that year sparked the Great Depression. The length and depth of that crisis helped

to stimulate the study of modern macroeconomics, with huge efforts to prevent a
repeat over the years ahead. Indeed, the failures of the response to the Great
Depression played a large role in the much swifter response to the 2008 financial
crisis, helping to avoid a second Great Depression.
That experience established much of the modern financial safety net that exists to
this day. For instance in the US, the Federal Deposit Insurance Corporation was

established in 1933. The US and many other countries came off the Gold Standard
as well in this period. There were also regulatory reforms, and the Glass-Steagall Act
separated out commercial from investment banking.
2. 1944-1971: The Bretton Woods era coincided with fewer financial
crises
Around the end of WWII, economic policymakers sought to create an international
monetary system for the post-war era. The relative financial calm that followed

reflected a combination of restrictions on international capital movement, tightly
regulated domestic financial systems, and a system of fixed exchange rates that
aimed to provide stability and prevent the competitive devaluations that occurred
in the 1930s. This saw exchange rates pegged against the US Dollar, which in turn
was convertible to gold at $35 per ounce.
At the same time, capital controls helped preserve monetary policy flexibility, while

maintaining the system of fixed exchange rates. These controls had several
downsides but they also insulated countries from “sudden stop” crises that can
occur, when capital rapidly flees a country. Moreover, capital controls prevented
the rapid buildup of cross-border systemic leverage. So it was more difficult to
generate a global financial crisis in the same way.

### Page 5

3 November 2026
Thematic Research
Deutsche Bank AG Page 5
Figure 2: Gold price (USD/oz, log scale)
10

1920 1940 1960 1980 2000 2020
In the Bretton Woods
era, the dollar was tied
to gold
Source : Finaeon, Deutsche Bank
Alongside capital controls, there were also domestic reforms in the aftermath of the
Great Depression, which sought to avoid a repeat. As mentioned earlier, the US
Glass-Steagall Act of 1933 separated commercial banking from investment

banking. Another example of the era’s regulated financial system in the US was
Regulation Q, which imposed maximum interest rates that commercial banks
could pay on deposits. That meant depositors could not search for yield and cause
a run on deposits as easily. Another rationale was that with institutions not
competing for deposits, it would avoid a compression in the margin between the
rates banks paid on their lending and borrowing, which would disincentivise a push

into riskier investments.
Finally, economic growth was also stronger in this period, with the period between
WWII and the 1973 oil shock often described as the “Golden Age”. This played a role
in avoiding financial crises too, as faster growth helped keep debt default rates
down, avoiding the kind of “trigger” events above that might spark a financial crisis.
Coupled with financial repression, strong growth also helped to bring down the
high post-war public debt burdens.

Over time however, the peg between the dollar and gold found itself under growing
strain. The economist Robert Triffin identified the “Triffin dilemma”. Supplying the
world with dollar reserves resulted in outstanding dollar liabilities around the world
that exceeded the gold reserves in the US, weakening confidence that dollars could
ultimately be redeemed for gold. Conversely, restricting dollar supply to protect
those reserves and preserve convertibility risked depriving the system of the

liquidity needed to support trade and growth.
These tensions intensified in the late 1960s amid expansionary US policies,
including spending on the Vietnam War and Great Society programmes. The
continued drain in US gold reserves eventually led the US to suspend the
convertibility of the US Dollar into gold, effectively ending the Bretton Woods
system. Initially, there were attempts to bring some kind of fixed-exchange rate
system back, but the world of fiat currencies (with no link to a precious metal)

persists to this day.

### Page 6

3 November 2026
Thematic Research
Page 6 Deutsche Bank AG
3. 1971-2008: Financial crises become more frequent after the
breakdown of Bretton Woods to the Global Financial Crisis
After the breakdown of the Bretton Woods system, financial crises became a more
regular occurrence. While the end of the gold convertibility did not by itself trigger
future crises, it opened the door for wider financial liberalisation, with the resulting
rise in capital mobility and looser domestic financial regulation then helping to

facilitate a global build-up of imbalances.
The 1980s and 1990s were a period of frequent crisis events. The transition away
from capital controls meant that capital could flow more freely across borders.
However, this could also cause a “sudden stop” if investors suddenly pulled their
capital out of a country, contributing to bank runs and currency collapses.
Moreover, many emerging markets borrowed in foreign currencies like the US
Dollar, meaning that a depreciation in their own currency would see this USD-

denominated debt surge in local currency terms. This meant it was a particular
problem if US interest rates were increasing, as it became harder for many
emerging markets to repay their debts. This was a contributing factor behind the
Latin American debt crisis in the early 1980s. And the rate hikes also had domestic
ramifications, being a contributing factor behind the Savings and Loan crisis in the
United States.
With capital flowing more freely, that also played a role in the 1994 Mexican Peso

Crisis, and the 1997 Asian Financial Crisis. A sudden reversal in capital flows led to
crisis and ultimately recession in many countries. The following year, the Russian
financial crisis then took place, and fears grew in the United States about the wider
ramifications, as the hedge fund Long-Term Capital Management (LTCM) came
under pressure. Indeed, this period saw 75bps of pre-emptive rate cuts from the
Federal Reserve.
This volatility also saw the Fed proactively seek to step in and smooth out the

economic volatility. This was broadly successful, but in the process also helped
engender complacency and a further buildup of leverage. Indeed, after the dot com
bubble burst, the federal funds rate was cut to just 1% in mid-2003, where it
remained until mid-2004.
This period saw clear optimism that another Great Depression was highly unlikely.
For instance, Nobel Prize-winning economist Robert Lucas argued in his 2003 AEA
Presidential Address that “macroeconomics in this original sense has succeeded:

Its central problem of depression prevention has been solved, for all practical
purposes, and has in fact been solved for many decades.”
However, as we now know, the 2008 financial crisis occurred, driving the biggest
global downturn since the Great Depression of the 1930s. It turned out that Minsky
had been correct, and a lengthy period without a crisis had once again helped to
induce complacency, laying the foundations for a repeat.

3 November 2026

Section 3: The Post-2008 world: Why have we not had
another big financial crisis?
Since the Global Financial Crisis of 2008, we have thankfully avoided a major
financial crisis. Despite clear moments of stress, most notably around the collapse
of SVB in March 2023, we have not seen a similar wave of bank collapses on that
scale. There are several components to this.
First, post-GFC deleveraging cleared out risks in the system, with countries and
sectors that had excess indebtedness before the GFC experiencing pronounced

declines in leverage.
Second, there have been reforms explicitly designed to avoid a repeat of 2008.
After the crisis happened, there was a clear recognition that gaps in regulation had
exacerbated the crisis, leading to effects that cascaded across the financial system.
For instance, the Basel III reforms required banks to hold more equity, with a
minimum requirement for Common Equity Tier 1 (CET1) capital. They also
introduced the liquidity coverage ratio, which requires banks to hold enough high-

quality liquid assets to ensure they can cover their net cash outflows under a 30-day
stressed scenario. In addition, the net stable funding ratio was brought in, requiring
banks to have sufficient stable funding that exceeded the required amount of stable
funding for a one-year stressed scenario.
At the national level, further reforms were also introduced. In the US, the Dodd-
Frank Act was introduced, with measures including the Volcker Rule that restricted

banks from engaging in proprietary trading, and required the Federal Reserve to
perform an annual stress test of large institutions. Meanwhile in the European
Union, which also experienced a sovereign debt crisis, reforms included a “Single
Supervisory Mechanism”, whereby banking supervision was brought under the
ECB, ensuring harmonised regulatory standards.

### Page 8

3 November 2026
Thematic Research
Page 8 Deutsche Bank AG
Third, the share of financial assets held by banks has fallen since the 2008 crisis.
This hasn’t eliminated risk, but commercial banks are funded by retail deposits that
can quickly fly. By contrast, non-bank financial institutions (NBFIs), e.g. hedge
funds, are not as prone to these bank run dynamics. For instance, a private credit
closed-ended fund is not one where investors can simply redeem their funds on

demand.
We can see this shift in the data. Looking at the FSB’s group of 21 plus the Eurozone,
we can see that the share of assets held by banks has fallen from a peak of 46% in
2008, to 38% in 2024. By contrast, the share held by NBFIs has risen from 43% to
51%.
Figure 5: Share of Assets of Financial Corporations (FSB 21 + EA Group)
35%
40%
45%

2002 2006 2010 2014 2018 2022
Banks Nonbank Financial Intermediation Sector (NBFI)
Source :Haver Analytics, Deutsche Bank

Fourth, the lessons of 2008 have meant that when the financial system has had
wobbles since, policymakers have acted with incredible speed to stop the
turmoil.
This was most obvious when the Covid-19 pandemic began. Whilst a pandemic
was very different to a financial crisis, there was initially huge turmoil in financial
markets. Indeed, the S&P 500 fell by more than a third in just over a month, a decline
at a speed and scale unseen since the Great Depression. Meanwhile, US HY credit

spreads soared to levels last seen around the financial crisis, and at the height of the
turmoil, there was a huge spike in real yields, despite the economy entering a
recession.
Against this backdrop, central banks wasted no time in acting. For instance, the Fed
delivered an emergency 50bp rate cut, followed by another 100bp rate cut.
Moreover, they swiftly resumed Quantitative Easing, and announced the
establishment of multiple new facilities to ensure the smooth functioning of credit

markets. These included a Primary Market Corporate Credit Facility, to support new
bond and loan issuance, along with a Secondary Market Corporate Credit facility,
which offered liquidity for outstanding corporate bonds. Meanwhile, a third facility,
called the Term Asset-Backed Securities Loan Facility, was set up to enable the
issuance of asset-backed securities. Similarly in Europe, the ECB set up a new
Pandemic Emergency Purchase Programme (PEPP), which ultimately had a total

envelope of €1.85tn.
Whilst the pandemic caused a huge economic shock, the swift action meant it did
not translate into a financial one, and the brief tightening in credit conditions was

### Page 9

3 November 2026
Thematic Research
Deutsche Bank AG Page 9
unwound over the coming months.
A similar pattern was seen during the regional banking turmoil of March 2023. This
was a financial panic, which initially began with the collapse of Silicon Valley Bank.
However, this began to spread, and Signature Bank also failed. Then in Europe,
Credit Suisse came under investor scrutiny and saw large deposit outflows, which
culminated in a purchase by UBS that included guarantees from the Swiss

government. This coincided with huge market moves, with the 2yr Treasury yield
posting its biggest daily decline in over 40 years, with a 61bps decline on March 13.
However, policymakers swiftly moved to stop any contagion. The US Treasury,
Federal Reserve and the FDIC released a joint statement stating that all deposits at
SVB would be protected, and that the Federal Reserve would provide additional
funding to ensure banks could meet the needs of depositors. Meanwhile, the Fed’s

balance sheet briefly expanded again, despite the fact it had begun the process of
quantitative tightening. These actions ultimately meant the crisis proved contained
and did not lead to a wave of further bank collapses in the manner of 2008.

Fifth, policymakers have also responded to recent shocks with huge fiscal

transfers, meaning that debt has migrated from private towards sovereign
balance sheets.
Whilst global debt has risen slightly over the last decade, if you dig deeper into the
numbers, that’s entirely driven by a rise in government debt. If we compare the
statistics for Q2 2026, relative to Q2 2016, there’s actually been a decline in debt for
households, financial corporations, and nonfinancial corporations. The only one of
the four segments that’s gone up in the last decade is government debt.

In relative terms, the transformation is even bigger if we look back to 2007, before
the Global Financial Crisis kicked off. Back then, government debt was the smallest
of the four categories, but because of the GFC and the pandemic, it’s now become
the biggest of the four categories.
That in turn has led to separate issues, with government bond yields at multi-year
highs, alongside rising net interest spending. So the fiscal space has become
limited. However, public balance sheets don’t face the same kind of system-wide

run that a bank would.

Overall, the post-2008 experience offers three key forward-looking lessons. First,
stronger bank balance sheets and the shift towards non-bank finance suggest

future stress may originate outside banks. As such, historical analyses centred on
banking crises may provide a less complete guide. Second, policymakers have
become increasingly effective at containing liquidity pressures and amplification.
And third, the shift in debt towards sovereign balance sheets has cushioned private-
sector shocks but narrowed the capacity for future intervention.
Section 4: What are potential candidates for the next crisis?

This section considers some of the current risks in the financial system and whether
they could be sowing the seeds for the next financial crisis. This section is not
designed to be a comprehensive list of financial risks, but rather an assessment of
three important areas where risks have risen in recent years.
1. Could AI spark broader vulnerabilities?
A huge driver of the current equity bull run has been the growth in AI-related stocks.
This report won’t explore the debate as to whether this is a bubble, but examine

whether a correction could eventually propagate into a systemic financial crisis. As
noted earlier, a reversal in the investment cycle need not cause a crisis in itself, and
the bursting of the dot com bubble also did not lead to a broader financial crisis.
Nevertheless, there are issues that could spark vulnerabilities. First, the rapid rise
in AI capex raises questions about whether the investment will pay off. The AI build-
out is on course to exceed the scale of past technological investment cycles, which

have often involved overinvestment even when the technologies ultimately proved
transformative. Second, funding is shifting from predominantly operating cash
flow towards external capital, and rising debt issuance in particular. Third, greater
equity-market concentration increases exposure to a reassessment of technology
valuations. Together, these developments make a correction a clear risk.
To assess the potential for systemic stress, we apply the framework in Greenwood,

Hanson, Shleifer and Sørensen (GHSS). This associates heightened crisis risk with
the combination of a strong run-up in asset prices and excessive credit growth,
estimating a 45% probability of a crisis within 3 years if the two thresholds in Figure
11 are met. On a backward-looking basis as of 2025, an economy-wide assessment
finds that the equity-price increase meets the framework’s “red zone” test, but
business credit growth has been considerably more subdued, given the slowdown

### Page 11

3 November 2026
Thematic Research
Deutsche Bank AG Page 11
in credit following the 2022-23 tightening cycle. That is, going into 2026, the pattern
was one of a displacement of credit from other interest-sensitive sectors towards
AI and technology rather than any broad-based rise in leverage.
Nevertheless, US data does show a clear acceleration in credit trends so far in 2026.
To make a more forward-looking assessment, we incorporate the rise in issuance

in 2026 and indicatively extend it into 2027 using the estimated rise in AI capex
plans, assuming credit growth elsewhere in the US economy remains stable
(footnote: We see two-sided risks to this – we may understate the broader credit
demand in tech adjacent areas; conversely, this may understate the drag of higher
rates on credit demand in non-AI related sectors). We also extend the estimated
2027 trend into 2028, including an assumption that equity prices continue their

trend over the past 12 months, to provide an illustrative scenario over the next two
years. Under these assumptions, we would see a visible rise in aggregate US
business sector leverage, leaving the sector just shy of economy-wide red-zone
levels by 2028. As such, approaching that territory by 2028 is conceivable if the AI
cycle continues to accelerate, both in terms of increased debt borrowing and a
renewed rise in valuations.
While the GHSS framework gives a useful quantification of the potential risks, its

indicators also warrant some qualification, which we judge to come in opposing
directions:
n On the one hand, there is a risk that aggregate credit measures may
understate vulnerabilities when borrowing is highly concentrated. Circular
financing, leases and off-balance-sheet structures complicate the picture,
and while we incorporate the recent rise in payables in our leverage metric
to capture some of this risk, it may well be underestimated. Moreover,

realised interest costs remain below current market borrowing rates after
the lengthy period of ultra-low rates, so leverage trends alone may miss the
pressure from rising debt-service costs.
n On the other hand, the price-based metric used by GHSS to assess excess
asset price growth (the 3-year real rise in equity prices) may overstate
valuation excesses given the strong rise in earnings. Indeed, P/E multiples
have already seen a sizeable correction since late 2025, which was most

pronounced for software stocks, and currently there is little evidence of
overly stretched tech valuations, especially when compared to the dot com
boom.
The risk of stress amplification is also likely to depend on the nature of any AI
correction. A downgrading of the AI capex cycle due to slower growth in AI
demand, financing constraints or supply bottlenecks may result in weaker
valuations, leading to negative wealth effects and slower capital expenditure.

However, while this would create an economic headwind, it need not produce a
systemic crisis. And while parallels to the dot com bubble may well be relevant,
currently there are factors that suggest downside risks would be more moderate.
For instance, operating cash flow still plays an important role in financing the AI
buildout, whilst there has been a limited run-up in borrowing and much less
stretched valuations relative to the late-1990s.
Our assessment therefore remains that an AI correction is a clearer risk than an AI-

led systemic crisis at this point. For these risks to become more material, it would
need leverage to keep building but AI returns to eventually disappoint, raising the
likelihood of credit events and amplifying the investment slowdown through the
real economy. We will focus on this interaction between debt-service pressures and

### Page 12

3 November 2026
Thematic Research
Page 12 Deutsche Bank AG
the role of credit events more in the next section.

2. Risks from higher rates and private credit
In the previous section we noted that modest economy-wide debt growth doesn’t
necessarily imply modest credit risk when debt-service costs are rising. As existing
debt reprices or is refinanced, borrowers can face greater pressure even without
additional borrowing. This is a key feature of the debt cycle, whereby vulnerabilities
accumulate during the expansion, and rising rates (often amid a monetary

tightening due to an overheating economy) can then expose them. These risks are
relevant in the context of the current rate hiking cycle, especially as much of the
catch up in debt service costs is yet to play out (see chart below).
With banking-sector risks having moderated since the GFC, the rapid expansion of
private credit and its opaque structure have understandably drawn attention as a
possible source of the next crisis. Parallels with the subprime credit boom have

been periodically made. However, we think these should not be overstated. Even
though the dollar size of the US private credit market is similar to that of subprime
around 2006-07, the market must be assessed relative to the economy (which has
doubled in nominal terms). Moreover, the slower build-up, restrictions on
redemptions and lower risks of direct wealth effects all suggest much more
moderate amplification risks than in 2008.
There are already signs that the private-credit growth cycle is slowing (see chart)

and our credit strategists have noted warning signs including weaker new inflows
into BDCs, increasing payment-in-kind interest and prospective refinancing
pressures. That said, defaults remain limited thus far so these should not be seen
as evidence of an imminent systemic event.

### Page 14

3 November 2026
Thematic Research
Page 14 Deutsche Bank AG
Whether more systemic risks could emerge will depend on issues of solvency,
opacity and capacity for intervention.
n First, while redemption gates can limit immediate cash outflows and
reduce the need to sell illiquid loans under pressure, they do not restore a
borrower’s ability to service debt or absorb underlying credit losses. This
distinction between liquidity protection and solvency is therefore central to

judging whether a slowing credit cycle remains orderly.
n Opacity remains a concern, both in financing structures and in the
distribution of exposures among end-holders, including insurers and other
non-banks. This can make losses harder to identify and potential
amplification harder to assess.
n Policymakers’ success in containing liquidity pressures may not translate
into an effective response to credit events - liquidity provision alone cannot

repair impaired balance sheets. Extending support to opaque non-bank
exposures raises difficult questions about eligibility, loss allocation and the
threshold for intervention.
Quite aside from private credit, the fact that we are back in a rate hiking cycle from
the major central banks also adds to the current risks. Indeed, higher rates bring
down inflation, in part because they tighten financial conditions. But a
consequence of this is that it often reveals vulnerabilities in the financial system,

and makes a crisis more likely. For instance, it was a contributing factor behind the
2023 regional banking crisis, as banks had growing unrealised losses on their
securities portfolio when rates began to rise again. So even if higher rates don’t
directly cause a crisis, they can often be a key contributor. Indeed, hiking cycles
have regularly coincided with some kind of shock, as the experience of recent
decades demonstrates.

3. Stretched fiscal positions and possible NBFI amplifiers
As discussed earlier, the migration of debt towards sovereign balance sheets has

helped cushion private-sector shocks, but this has come at the cost of higher fiscal
risks. Higher public debt and sovereign yields have limited the room for fiscal
stimulus, while persistent inflation has limited the room for monetary stimulus. This
makes another round of coordinated macroeconomic support harder to deliver.
The issue with high public debt is that it if fiscal sustainability is questioned, that can
raise sovereign borrowing costs, which can amplify existing private-sector

vulnerabilities via a tightening in financial conditions and slower growth.
The financial risk posed by sovereign stress will also depend on the financial
amplification channels. These were clear in the European sovereign crisis with the
bank-sovereign loop, but the changing structure of sovereign markets suggests
that the sovereign–NBFI feedback loop may now warrant increasing attention. This
was visible with the 2022 LDI crisis in the UK, while a more recent concern is the run

up in leveraged hedge fund positions in sovereign bond markets, with hedge funds’
sovereign bond exposures more than doubling since 2022 (see chart below). As
noted also by the IMF, this reflects greater issuance and a rising share of sovereign
debt held by hedge funds.
The lessons from Black Monday, LTCM and the LDI crisis offer reassurance that
intervention can contain market stress originating outside the banking system. But
they are not a guarantee that future liquidity shocks will remain economically

benign. The greater scale and interconnectedness of non-bank finance mean that
we can’t simply rely on historical examples today.
Looking forward, a combination of sovereign repricing, leveraged financial
amplification and a more constrained policy response risks proving harder to
resolve than past episodes.

Conclusion
Whilst a major financial crisis has been avoided since the GFC and the subsequent
sovereign crisis, the problem of financial instability has yet to be permanently

solved. On the one hand, reforms to the financial system and the swift actions of
policymakers have helped to prevent a repeat. But as we know from Hyman
Minsky’s warning, lengthy periods of stability can often lay the foundations for the
next crisis, even if these take place slowly. Indeed, risks often shift rather than
disappear.
Looking forward, there are several vectors of risk worth paying attention to. But
financial crises often start in areas where people are paying little attention, or risks

are assumed to be contained. Indeed, it is the areas where risk is underappreciated
which often prove the most vulnerable.
As such, predicting where the next crisis might arise is inherently difficult. But the
topic is a vital one, given the long-lasting damage that financial crises can cause
when they do occur. Indeed, with the fiscal space for sovereigns increasingly
constrained today, the margin for a future policy error is limited.

民生必需品：P&G有機成長 +4.0%中，銷量貢獻 +2.4%，

的連鎖與咖啡系統維持正成長，倚賴折價促銷的速食與披
薩鏈許多為負成長。在外食價格比居家食品高出約1.7個
百分點的環境下，客流與價格的取捨將是4Q26餐飲評等
的主要變數。
《成本與定價動能》
9月CPI顯示，整體CPI月增 +0.6%、年增 +3.7%，核心
CPI年增 +2.6%；能源年增 +20.4%、汽油年增 +34.2%，

是整體通膨上行的主因。對核心消費而言，關鍵在於食品
與能源的分離：居家食品月增 −0.1%、年增 +1.8%，外
食年增 +3.5%。
對毛利的含意：能源成本上升而零售端食品價格溫和，代
表核心消費業者較難以轉嫁成本，壓力更可能先反映在毛
利率而非銷售額。P&G在指引中揭露本財年約14億美元
稅後成本逆風（含其他逆風合計約每股0.79美元，約拖累
核心EPS成長11%），為公司自行揭露數字，本行未重新
計算。Kimberly-Clark則預期2026年有機銷售成長約落
後 所 屬 品 類 加 權 成 長 100bp ， 顯示在品類成長僅約
+1.7% 的環境下價格與組合的操作空間有限。成長動能
偏向「量」而非「價」；此結構有利於營收穩定，但對毛
利率的支撐較弱。

## Document 508: タイGDP（26年7-9月期）～成長率は2.9％に加速、電子関連輸出と設備投資がけん引

Source PDF: all report pdf/タイGDP（26年7-9月期）～成長率は2.9％に加速、電子関連輸出と設備投資がけん引.pdf
Report source: NLI
Date: 2026-11-16
Pages: 3
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

【結果概要】 
タイの2026年7-9月期の実質 GDP成長率（速報値）1は前年同期比 2.9％となり、前期の同 2.4％
（改定値）から加速した（図表１）。Bloomberg調査の市場予想2（3.1％）を小幅に下回ったものの、
前年同期比では２四半期ぶりに伸びが高まった。また、季節調整済み前期比成長率は 0.3％となり、
前期のほぼ横ばい（0.0％）から小幅に加速した。 
需要項目別にみると、AI・データセンター関連の世界的な需要拡大を背景に輸出が加速し、その
伸び率は鈍化した輸入を上回った。民間投資も機械・設備投資を中心に一段と加速したほか、民間
消費は自動車購入の増加を受けて持ち直し、公共投資も増加に転じた。一方、政府消費や民間建設
投資は減少した。 
NESDCは2026年通年の成長率見通しを従来の2.0～2.5％から 2.5％へ上方修正した。また、2027
年の成長率は2.0～3.0％（中央値 2.5％）と予想した。

【支出別の詳細】 
民間消費は前年同期比 2.5％となり、前期（2.1％）から加速した（図表２）。内訳をみると、耐
久財は 12.6％と前期（3.7％）から大幅に加速した。特に自動車購入が 20.9％と高い伸びを示した
ほか、非耐久財も 2.6％と小幅に加速した。一方、半耐久財は 3.0％、サービス消費は 0.8％といず

### Page 2

【産業別の詳細】 
産業別にみると、農林水産業は前期から小幅に鈍化した一方、鉱工業は製造業を中心に加速し、
サービス業は前期と同じ伸びを維持した。 
農林水産業は前年同期比 1.3％となり、前期（1.4％）から小幅に鈍化した（図表４）。ゴムや野
菜、豚などの生産が増加し、漁業も７四半期ぶりに増加に転じた一方、コメやアブラヤシ、果物は
減産となった。 
鉱工業は前年同期比3.6％となり、前期（2.3％）から加速した。製造業は 3.7％と前期（1.6％）
から加速し、16四半期ぶりの高い伸びとなった。電子部品・基板や自動車、石油製品などの生産増
加が押し上げた。一方、鉱業は 1.8％と前期（5.5％）から減速し、電気・ガス業も 3.7％と前期（6.2％）
から鈍化した。 
サービス業は前年同期比 2.6％となり、前期と同じ伸びを維持した。小売・卸売業は 6.0％と前
（図表３）

### Page 3

【景気の見方と今後の注目点】 
7-9月期のタイ経済は前年同期比2.9％と前期（2.4％）から加速し、季節調整済み前期比も 0.3％
と前期のほぼ横ばいから持ち直した。AI・データセンター関連の需要拡大を背景に電子機器の輸出
が伸び、機械・設備投資も好調だった。製造業や小売・卸売業が加速した一方、民間消費は自動車
購入がけん引し、サービス消費は鈍化した。宿泊・飲食業や建設業にも弱さが残っており、景気回
復の裾野はなお広がりを欠いている。 
先行きの焦点は、好調な輸出と民間投資が国内経済にどこまで波及するかである。NESDCは2026
年の成長率見通しを従来の 2.0～2.5％から 2.5％へ上方修正した。電子機器などへの需要拡大を背
景に、財輸出額（ドルベース）の伸びを 15.1％から 20.4％、民間投資を 9.6％から 14.1％へ引き
上げた一方、民間消費は 2.6％から 2.3％へ下方修正した。輸出や設備投資の拡大に伴い中間財・
資本財の輸入も増えており、こうした好調さが幅広い国内企業の雇用や所得の改善につながるかが
注目される。 
2027年は輸出・投資の増勢鈍化が見込まれる。NESDCは同年の成長率を2.0～3.0％（中央値2.5％）
と予想している。世界経済・貿易の減速などを背景に、財輸出額の伸びは2026年の20.4％から8.6％、
民間投資は 14.1％から 7.9％へ低下する見通しだ。輸出・民間投資の増勢鈍化が見込まれるなか、
公共投資や観光の回復、純輸出の改善が景気をどこまで下支えするかが注目される。 
リスク面では、米国の通商政策や中東情勢の不確実性に加え、世界的な AI関連投資の減速に注
意が必要である。AI関連需要が弱まれば、足元の成長を支える電子機器輸出や設備投資が下押しさ
れるおそれがある。また、エルニーニョに伴う干ばつは農業生産や農家所得を圧迫する可能性があ
り、高水準の家計債務も消費回復の重石となる。こうしたリスクが顕在化し、内需の回復が遅れれ
ば、景気の下押し圧力が強まる可能性がある。

本資料記載のデータは各種の情報源から入手・加工したものであり、その正確性と完全性を保証するものではありません。 
また、本資料は情報提供が目的であり、記載の意見や予測は、いかなる契約の締結や解約を勧誘するものではありません。

## Document 516: ブラジルの「トリプル安」は一過性の混乱にとどまるか ～ルラ氏の逆転勝利で財政懸念が再燃、硬直的な歳出構造が信認回復の足かせに～

Source PDF: all report pdf/ブラジルの「トリプル安」は一過性の混乱にとどまるか ～ルラ氏の逆転勝利で財政懸念が再燃、硬直的な歳出構造が信認回復の足かせに～.pdf
Report source: Unknown
Date: 未知日期
Pages: 5
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

World Trends

2026 年10 月 27 日

1/5 
ブラジルの「トリプル安」は一過性の混乱にとどまるか 
～ルラ氏の逆転勝利で財政懸念が再燃、硬直的な歳出構造が信認回復の足かせに～ 
 経済調査部 主席エコノミスト  西濵 徹 
（℡：050-5474-7495）

（要旨） 
○ ブラジルでは、10 月 25 日の大統領選挙の決選投票において現職のルラ大統領が逆転勝利を
収めたことを受けて、翌26 日の金融市場では株式、通貨レアル、国債が同時に売られる「ト
リプル安」に直面した。背景には、右派フラビオ氏の勝利による財政健全化や金利低下への
期待を過度に織り込んだ取引が巻き戻されたことに加え、ルラ政権下で財政健全化の取り組
みが遅れるとの懸念がある。 
○ 同国では、政府債務の大きさや義務的経費の割合の高さが財政運営の制約となるなか、レア
ル安によるインフレ圧力の高まりが中銀の追加利下げ余地を狭めるとともに、金利上昇が景
気や財政を一段と圧迫するリスクが懸念される。一方、十分な外貨準備など対外ショックに
対する一定の耐性を有するほか、当局も市場安定化策を講じており、過度な相場変動が落ち
着く余地はある。 
○ ただし、市場の信認回復には具体的な財政健全化への取り組みが不可欠である。今後は、次
期政権の経済チームの人事や 2027 年度予算の内容、議会との合意形成を通じて、財政健全化
に向けた道筋を示せるかが重要となる。

### Page 2

2/5 
【ルラ氏の逆転勝利を受けて、金融市場においてブラジル資産を売却する動きが広がる】 
ブラジルでは、10 月 25 日に実施された大統領選挙の決選投票において、左派ＰＴ（労働者党）
から出馬した現職のルラ大統領が、右派
ＰＬ（自由党）のフラビオ・ボルソナロ
上院議員を破り、再選を決めた（注１）。
ブラジル高等選挙裁判所（ＴＳＥ）が公
表した最終結果によれば、ルラ氏の得票
率は 51.79％、フラビオ氏は 48.21％と
なった。これを受けて、翌 26 日の金融
市場では、株式、通貨レアル、国債のす
べてに売り圧力が強まった（図１）。株式
市場では、主要株価指数（ボベスパ指数）
が前営業日比で一時▲17％以上下落し、
取引を一時停止するサーキットブレーカーが発動された。為替市場においてもレアルが大きく下落
するとともに、債券市場では国債利回りや金利先物市場での金利上昇が進むなど、「トリプル安」に
直面した。

【右派政権への交代を過度に織り込んだ取引の巻き戻しが、相場変動を増幅させた】 
市場が大幅に変動した背景には、決選投票の前に積み上がった政権交代への期待が剥落したこと
がある。10 月４日の第１回投票では、事前予想に反してフラビオ氏が首位となった（注２）。これ
を受けて、その後の市場では、同氏の勝利によって財政健全化が進み、将来的な金利低下につなが
るとの期待が強まる動きがみられた（注３）。決選投票直前には、ボベスパ指数は第１回投票前に比
べて約７％、レアルも対ドルで約５％上昇していた。 
投資家の間では、フラビオ氏の勝利を受けた財政規律の強化や経済改革が進む可能性を織り込み、
株式やレアルを買うとともに、金利低下を見込む取引を進めていた。しかし、ルラ氏の逆転勝利に
よってこうした前提が崩れ、複数の市場で同時に持ち高を調整する動きが強まったと考えられる。
よって、現職の続投という結果にもかかわらず、市場が政権交代を強く見込んでいたことで、大幅
な価格調整に繋がった。今回の下落幅を考える際には、ルラ政権の政策に対する評価に加え、選挙
前の取引が特定の結果に偏っていた可能性を考慮する必要がある。このため、市場では右派政権に
よる政策転換への期待が過度に先行していたと考えられる。

注１ 10 月26 日付レポート「ブラジル大統領選、ルラ氏が逆転勝利も政権運営には難路」 
  （https://www.dlri.co.jp/report/macro/666866.html） 
注２ 10 月５日付レポート「ブラジル大統領選は決選投票へ、フラビオ氏が首位」 
  （https://www.dlri.co.jp/report/macro/666284.html） 
注３ 10 月６日付レポート「金融市場はブラジルの右派回帰を好感、株価・レアルともに上昇」 
  （https://www.dlri.co.jp/report/macro/666333.html）

### Page 3

3/5

【市場が抱く懸念には、公的財務の大きさに加え、硬直的な歳出増による財政運営の難しさがある】 
市場がルラ氏の再選に厳しい反応を示した背景には、社会給付などの拡充が続くことにより、財
政健全化の取り組みが遅れるとの懸念
がある。2026 年８月末時点における一般
政府の債務残高（グロス値）はＧＤＰ比
82.9％に達している。さらに、ここ数年
の同国では、インフレによる高金利が常
態化するなか、利払い負担の大きさが財
政運営を難しくしている。さらに、近年
は司法判断に基づく支払債務（プレカト
リオス）の増大も歳出を圧迫しており、
財政運営の硬直性を一段と強める要因
となっている。 
こうした状況では、新たな恒久歳出に見合う財源を確保できるかが重要になる。財政の持続可能
性に対する懸念が強まれば、投資家は国債の保有により高い利回りを求める。この結果、政府の新
規調達や借換えに伴う負担が増え、財政健全化を一段と困難にする可能性がある。株式、通貨、債
券が同時に売られたことは、将来の政策運営に対する不確実性が、ブラジル資産全体に求められる
リスクの上乗せ幅を拡大させたと解釈できる。 
財政運営の硬直性は、2027 年度予算案において、基礎的歳出に占める義務的経費の割合が91.7％
に上ることにも現れている。このため、裁量的な歳出削減による財政健全化の実現のハードルは極
めて高いと考えられる。今後は、政府が掲げる財政目標に加え、前述したプレカトリオスなど例外
措置を含めた実際の歳出、恒久財源の確保、債務残高の見通しを確認することが重要になる。ルラ
政権が主導する社会政策の効果を見極めつつ、給付の対象や制度設計、税制優遇、支出の優先順位
を見直すことができるかが、財政運営への信認を左右することが予想される。

【レアル安が利下げ余地を狭める一方、金利上昇が景気と財政を圧迫する懸念も残る】 
今回の混乱が、金融政策の先行きに影響を与える可能性にも注意が必要である。中銀は９月の定
例会合（COPOM）において、政策金利である Selic を 13.75％に引き下げた（注４）。一方で、会合
後に公表された声明においては、持続的な通貨下落やインフレ期待の不安定化、消費刺激策などを
物価の上振れリスクに挙げるとともに、財政政策が金融政策や資産価格に及ぼす影響を注視する姿
勢を示した。

注４ ９月17 日付レポート「ブラジル中銀は５会合連続の利下げも、緩和継続には慎重姿勢」 
  （https://www.dlri.co.jp/report/macro/665843.html）

### Page 4

4/5 
足元では、イラン情勢の悪化を受けた原油高などを背景に、鈍化基調が続いてきたインフレ率が
再び加速に転じる兆しがみられる（図
３）。こうしたなか、レアル安の動きが長
期化すれば、輸入品や輸入原材料の価格
上昇を通じて、物価への押し上げ圧力が
強まることが予想される。さらに、企業
や家計部門におけるインフレ予想が上
方修正されれば、中銀が追加利下げを進
める余地は狭まる。次回の COPOM は 11
月３～４日に予定されており、選挙後の
為替変動や期待インフレの動きをどう
評価するかが注目される。中銀として
は、レアル安の継続性のほか、物価への波及、国内需要の減速などを総合的に判断する必要がある。 
実体経済への影響としては、企業と家計の資金調達環境が厳しくなることが懸念される。企業の
借入費用や債務の借換え負担が増えれば、利益や設備投資に下押し圧力がかかる。家計向け融資の
金利上昇は、住宅や耐久消費財の需要を抑える要因となる。こうした金融環境の悪化が長期化すれ
ば、景気減速による税収の伸び悩みと利払い負担の増加が重なり、財政運営をさらに難しくするこ
とも考えられる。財政運営に対する懸念が金利を押し上げるとともに、その金利上昇が景気と財政
を圧迫するという循環を抑えられるかが、今後の重要な課題となる。

【当局の市場安定化策と対外面での「緩衝材」はあるが、財政運営への信認回復が不可欠】 
市場の混乱を受けて、当局は国債市場の安定化に向けた対応を進めている。26 日には固定利付国
債や物価連動国債の買い戻しを実施するとともに、27 日に予定していた物価連動国債と 29 日の固
定利付国債の入札を中止する一方、27 日の政策金利連動国債の入札は維持した。これらの措置は、
売り注文が集中した市場において買い手を確保する一方、急激な価格変動を抑えることに役立つと
考えられる。ただし、その効果を持続させるには、中期的な財政改善策を示すとともに、投資家が
国債を保有しやすい環境を整えることが必要になる。
